# Stellar Blade Gore Mod: technical notes

Corpse-combat audit (November 7): `hits_after.lua` considers each fresh tracked enemy and each moving blade in speed order until a contact dispatches; the tracker supplies its own weapon names to classification. The existing 0.4 s post-death window, per-attacker rearm, speed, montage and geometric contact guards remain. Under slow motion the minimum frame interval applies to real elapsed time before converting speed to game time, including the no-montage relative-speed gate. `enemies.lua` advances forced attack playback and swept lunge by game time, and main resets the capped diagnostics each death. A vertical downward mesh hit without horizontal push survives the fallback and can smash; a randomly chosen downward fallback angle alone is not stomp evidence. The available 23:01:33 log instead shows such a guessed 70-degree direction was misclassified as a stomp and did smash; it does not identify the reported no-smash event. `death_evidence` simulates vertical evidence and two pre-ragdoll skill hits, but the single-player log reports no skill hook calls, so actual mesh timing and the no-smash cause require another playtest. `hits_after`, `after_ai`, `after`, `after_nomontage`, `after_geometry`, `hit_classification`, `odds` and `smash_hits` cover the fake-game behavior. Attacks After Death stays experimental and off by default; enable it for a corpse-combat test. The older historical descriptions below may describe previous settings.

Cape detach: `g` now sends the already-cut limb as a gone root and the kept accessory roots for this `MODE_CUT` only. The limb subtree excludes those chains; mixed vertices give up the old limb's weights and go to the body side, while vertices wholly on the old limb remain intact. This does not turn the `n` sink-exclusion list into a weight-drop list. The right-cape real-mesh test injects the observed mixed weights on a visible triangle: before, flinging the arm adds three visible stretched triangles; afterward it adds none. This models the reported weights rather than reproducing the exact in-game pose; the fix needs a playtest.

Experimental spawner follow-up: group state is saved as a boolean map and kept by menu pruning; absent group IDs default folded. Tracking runs even with enemy gore and pushes off and matches already-followed pooled actors. A request not matched within ten seconds is logged as unconfirmed; this does not establish why the game did not create an actor. Passive repeatedly stops brains, releases controllers and stops montages and movement; leaving Passive re-possesses and restarts saved brains. Its in-game behavior still needs verification. Portraits use memory-only full object paths; monster album textures live under `Album/Native/image`, the Royal Guard uses Rael's `Album/NPC/image` texture, and missing or unknown paths are logged. The Airborne Squad's album image has not been identified.

Roster gap, closed: the Spawner now lists every one of the base game's 644 CharacterTable rows (generated into `roster.lua` from the game's table dump), grouped by tribe after the curated Naytiba/Angels/Bosses groups. Rows without a Blueprint are adopted by proximity, tracked and hostility-patched, but never cut; Eve herself is never adopted as a spawn. Insert cycles within the pick's group.

Enemy-gore coverage, widened: the Naytiba kinds grew from 10 to 27 with every other upright Bip001 humanoid the asset data supports (Skull Juggernaut, Crickets A/B/C, Gorillas, Sawsharks, Weapon Master, Openers, Hedge Boars, Beholder, Statue D, Marionette), each cut offline on its own body mesh except the Marionette's waist (`native/sbslice/test/real`, ENEMY=1: arm, head and waist, headless variants arm and waist; Marionette limb and head pass, its waist drags 27k leg verts with the upper body, so `noTorso` keeps it limb-and-head only: rolled and fallback torso becomes a limb, the blade vetoes the waist for a crossed limb instead). Asset-verified exclusions: the bosses (Elder phases, Raven Beast: sandbox-boss treatment, not plain kinds), the non-upright (Behemoths, Bots, Grubs, Antlions, Mites, Crawlers and other partial skeletons: quadruped/crawler bind poses), M_Mann and the ExoSuit (no physics asset to ragdoll on), the Bot upper body alone, the hunchbacks (no Bip001-Head), the VIP guard (head bones are hair helpers only). Known minor: waist cuts on the Gorillas and Sawsharks stretch 2-5 rim triangles when the torso separates ~50 cm or more (any plane/tilt; limb and head cuts clean); in-game the rim sits under the cap on a corpse that dissolves in seconds, still to verify in playtest. Portrait lookup now also matches `_BP` and `_BluePrint` asset suffixes (Crickets, Behemoth-row assets).


The November 5 evening session (7 deaths, CH_M_NA_39/31 on CH_P_EVE_10 then CH_P_EVE_09) ended in a vertical split whose headless half reportedly flew ~50 m high after the log's last line (the log ends at the death-screen animation, ~14 s in, right at recovery time: both halves were released at 200 cm/s and settled normally at 2.98 s, so the flight itself is not in the log). A run stop now notes any body still over 2000 cm/s on her or the split copy, so the next log catches it. Same session: on CH_P_EVE_09 an arm death-cut severed cape link Ab_CapeL02 mid-chain and the L chain was skipped at ragdoll start ("cape left as the cut left it"), leaving it unmanaged — the regular cape-stretch sightings on cuts with this outfit. No constraint or mass change was made without a playtest to verify it; the candidate is releasing or re-hanging the affected chain when its cut lands.

The November 5 session log (11 deaths, CH_M_NA_43/31/39, mod installed from this tree) showed death ticks averaging 3-8 ms of mod work with spikes to 20-70 ms (spin caps, follow-up analysis, blood/pool spawns, a death-screen widget scan), character-seed walks of 20-53 ms on level loads, and frame hitches to 1346 ms mostly with the mod's last tick at 1 ms (engine streaming and garbage collection, 10 passes in the worst minute). `fewerGC` now defaults on and the death-screen class scan is deferred from 1 s to 3 s into the death; per-tick timing marks tables/menu/held and the tick's first part separately so the next log attributes the remaining unmarked spikes. Spin/linear caps, trail sampling and attack checks are unchanged pending playtest measurement. A cut wider than 40 cm across now logs a `cut stretch` line (two thigh after-cuts in the session qualified at 228 and 45 cm, both on legs whose calf was already detached elsewhere); the native geometry is unchanged.

The November 5 playtest logged cut stages of 18-44 ms, cold sound lookups up to 33 ms, a 32 ms continuous wound spawn, and smash debris batches up to 36 ms. Timed-out asset requests could be removed from the fallback queue before the load window opened. They now remain queued and receive a paced fallback before new requests; this moves some first-use loads to background work but does not eliminate native cut or spawn costs. Ordinary smash batches now spawn one chunk per update; torso and full-body batches spawn two, with all chunks retained. The measured impact and timing remain to be tested in game.

Stumps use only the 0.5.5 `NS_Hit_Flesh_Slice_BloodOnly` pulse, retaining scale, orientation, restart cadence and duration. The additional `NS_A_BloodLoop` layer and its preload are removed; separate smash-impact blood remains. The selector for no-cut struggles can attempt rises while sideways and curl while face down, and chooses a first available act before allowing a rest. `struggle_selection` reproduces the logged sideways idle case and checks a prone case. Actual appearance, movement and performance require playtesting.

`blade_decision.lua` owns blade-plane selection, pose-based cut decisions and follow-up selection; queued work, aim state and cancellation remain in the gore lifecycle and are obtained when used. The no-split blade, odds, follow-up and split simulations exercise fake-game decisions; visual placement and weapon behavior require a playtest.

`gore_smash.lua` groups smash dispatch, hiding and restoring pieces, chunks and blood effects. The owning lifecycle supplies current delayed-work and actor lists, which recovery can replace. Focused smash, effect, stress and enemy-owner simulations pass; real-game appearance and timing need verification.

`low_arm.lua` groups low-health limb loss, action restrictions, regrowth and camp restoration. Shared `hm` and `screen` state remains available to death and world cleanup, while settings, gore service and hit evidence are obtained at each operation. `lowarm`, `regrow`, `campreload` and `piece` exercise the fake-game transitions; actual gameplay and object lifetimes need a playtest.

Weapon tracking and its per-world bone cache live in `swing_tracking.lua`; main schedules tracking and retains the selected swing. Selection lives in `swing_selection.lua` and receives live settings and object guards. `swing_perf` compares selection with an independent geometry calculation and checks settings reload. Actual weapon timing requires an in-game playtest.

Pool tracker insertion and the post-growth tracker loop reacquire their lists after engine callbacks; smash-profile and regrowth reads also occur at the original points of use. A recovery triggered inside fake decal-shield and pool-growth callbacks replaces those lists; `enemy_tracker` checks the current list is used. Both checks fail when their respective refresh is removed. Focused pool, smash, regrow, split and reload tests pass; actual callback re-entry and engine lifetimes need game verification.

`gore_smash_select.lua` owns smash target ranking, circular impact coverage, remaining-flesh profiles and completed-smash queries. It reads the current cut list because cleanup can replace it. It uses the existing cut-state, pose, bone and tuning helpers; smash dispatch and effects are in `gore_smash.lua`, while their queues and final cleanup remain in the gore lifecycle. `smash`, `smash_area`, `smash_hits`, `smash_pose`, `smash_effects`, `smash_lifecycle`, `blast` and `rock` pass. Actual hit geometry and visual outcome require game verification.

`ue4ss/check_scripts.sh` is the shared Lua syntax and unexpected-global check used by installation and the simulation runner. This catches missing module dependencies before a guarded runtime call can silently discard the error. `script_preflight` checks valid Lua and rejection of syntax errors and missing globals in isolated scratch directories; when the Lua compiler is absent the check reports a skip rather than claiming verification.

`gore_watch.lua` owns the paced alive-time bone scan, pool-refill scheduling and asset preload queue. It retains the existing scan, async and sound-delay rules and reads current guards, context and pending cut state at call time. During extraction a missing command dependency was caught by `asyncload`; that test now calls the watch directly through scan cycles to detect errors the main-loop try wrapper suppresses. Focused preload, cut-list, effects, stage, reload, voice and character-cache checks pass; real preload costs still require a game test.

`gore_scheduler.lua` owns per-frame cut stages, queued cuts, impact-work priority and bleed restarts. It reads current queues through callbacks and refreshes after copy, impact and bleed callbacks, preserving re-entry and cleanup replacement behavior; pending state stays in the gore lifecycle. `scheduler_reentry` checks a bleed callback that schedules a cut, and fails when the post-callback refresh is removed. Focused stage, smash, split, lid, hit, ownership and reload simulations pass. Actual timing, frame cost and cut appearance need an in-game test.

`gore_pools.lua` owns floor decal creation and eviction, tracked openings, growth, bounded checks and cleanup. The main gore lifecycle supplies current delayed-work, tracker, decal and shield lists and setters because player/enemy cleanup may replace them; tracker ownership is still assigned before front insertion. Deferred callbacks reacquire current lists and guards. `smash_effects`, `enemy_tracker`, `smash_lifecycle`, `stains`, `stages`, `split`, `reload` and `lowarm` pass; real pools, cleanup, object lifetimes and frame costs require a game test.

`gore_floor.lua` owns floor traces, wall clearance and incremental ring checks. `gore_pools.lua` owns its trackers, decals and scheduling; `smash_effects` checks bounded trace counts, wall margins and gap rejection. Focused smash, stage, split, low-health and reload simulations pass; real geometry, visual continuity and frame cost require an in-game test.

`gore_native.lua` owns the Lua-to-sbslice startup handshake, request/reply command, fire-and-forget command and per-frame seam hold. It reads live guards, context and cut lists, preserving early exits, command timing, the cached cheat object and copy invalidation. The `gore_native` simulation checks handshake state, copy invalidation, seam dispatch and failure handling; focused preload, stage, split, reload, character-cache, effects and regrowth simulations pass, as does the native `alive` check. Real handshake, frame timing and engine lifetime still require an in-game test.

`gore_regrow.lua` owns regrowth copy creation, material transitions, sink handoff and cleanup. It obtains the current cut and clone lists and forced-LOD table when needed because recovery can replace them, and gets initialized object guards at call time. `regrow`, `campreload`, `lowarm`, `reload`, `piece`, `stages` and `split` pass. Rendered dissolve, actual engine lifetimes and recovery need in-game verification.

`gore_ownership.lua` owns `enemy.run`, owner lookup and guarded enemy-object access. A live list supplier is called for each owned operation because player and enemy cleanup replace shared lists; tagging retains its existing order, nested-owner restoration and error propagation. `enemy_tracker` checks replaced-list tagging, removal and owner restoration on callback errors in addition to chunk tracker isolation. Focused smash, split, reload, bosses and follow-up checks pass; real enemy object lifetime needs an in-game test.

`body_blood.lua` owns the legacy skin-blood ramp, original material values and recovery. World cleanup saves only the old values for restoration on the new pawn, without touching stale objects; the gore tick calls the module to restore them once the new pawn is valid. `body_blood` tests ramp, restore, world-change handoff and stains compatibility; `stains`, `reload`, `stages`, `split`, `smash_effects` and `lowarm` pass. In-game appearance and engine object lifetimes still need verification.

`gore_assets.lua` owns guarded asset lookup and the disposable class/default-object cache. World cleanup calls its reset while retaining rooted package-object guards; each retained wrapper is still validated before reuse. The module reads current context and guards at call time because the gore module initializes them later. Focused preload, reload, effects, stage and voice scenarios pass; actual object lifetime and loading costs require an in-game test.

Enemy-owned blood trackers were tagged by `enemy.run` after a callback by scanning appended entries, but smash-chunk trackers are inserted at the front to prioritize body openings. A front insertion could therefore leave its tracker unowned. Tracker creation now records the current owner directly, before insertion. `enemy_tracker` reproduced the missing tag and failed enemy cleanup before the fix; it now also checks player recovery preserves an enemy tracker. `smash_effects`, `smash_lifecycle`, `split` and `reload` pass. In-game enemy destruction and blood pools need verification.

`gore_preload.lua` installs native rooting and async request/poll methods on the existing native state. The scheduler remains in `gore.lua` while guarded asset lookup lives in `gore_assets.lua`; rooted state survives world changes as before. `try` and `valid` are read on each operation because the gore module receives them only during initialization. Preload timing, game object lifetime and frame cost need in-game verification.

The Lua simulation inventory contains 89 default scenarios and one intentionally parked diagonal scenario. `nosplits_blade` now verifies the public cut records, no split, and cleanup for head/torso redirection, including after-death hits, instead of checking only a log message. The `nosplits` random-death fixture previously passed on cut log lines even though its fake native helper returned an error for every cut; it now supplies a successful response and checks the resulting limb, absence of a split, and respawn cleanup. The fall test now checks the cut state for a fall and landing blow, and the low-health arm test checks the resulting arm for its initial left, right and random cases. The regular follow-up fixture had also allowed a blunt torso smash to override its intended limb-only initial death; both follow-up fixtures now disable those overrides and check actual cut records. The F6, outfit and staged-cut fixtures now check actual cut records instead of treating a cut log line as proof. The smash lifecycle fixture also checks recovery cancels unfinished staged cut work and does not reopen it. The alternate-character voice fixture previously exercised failed fake cuts; it now supplies a successful response and confirms a limb cut. The camera and custom-ragdoll fixtures still permit failed fake cut calls because their assertions target camera and compatibility, not cut success. The animated-death fixture now checks actual cut records and constraint calls for its no-cut and waist-recovery cases. `animdeath`, `fall` and `lowarm` still contain many log-based assertions and need further behavioral review.

`photo_guard.lua` owns the cut/ragdoll photo shield and active photo watch. It retains the existing polling interval, ragdoll and capsule holds, snapshot unlock, collision restore and weapon-trail reset; the surrounding screen state remains shared with recovery and other photo controllers. Actual game physics and photo transitions need verification.

`photo_visibility.lua` manages photo-mode visibility tracking, close-camera unhide behavior and near-clip restoration. Its state remains on the shared screen object, and it reads current gore and tuning through callbacks. Existing hidden-piece exclusions and photo close/recovery cleanup remain; in-game visibility and clip behavior need verification.

`photo_freeze.lua` groups photo view and pause detection, frozen mesh/hair handling, widget tracking and detached-head morph updates. Shared screen state remains available to recovery and world cleanup, and the module reads current pawn, gore and ragdoll state through callbacks. Game-side photo posing, hair, freeze timing and cleanup need verification.

`photo_controls.lua` owns photo activation, player/controller input and death-hold toggling and release. Existing key registration remains in main; live callbacks supply lifecycle state, settings, pawn, ragdoll and elapsed time. The per-frame controller path checks for an active death before reading settings or the pawn. Real input and photo behavior require a game test.

`death_screen.lua` owns the death/revive visibility rules, paced hook registration, screen-state reset, periodic probe, intro fade and animation handling. The main loop supplies elapsed death time to the probe. State remains on the shared screen object and world cleanup still discards stale widget references; callbacks read the current pawn, death state and settings. Screen appearance, hook behavior and object lifetimes require an in-game test.

`photo_collision.lua` owns photo-mode collision capture and restoration for the body, cloth meshes and capsule. The snapshot stays on the shared screen state, so failed photo entry, recovery and world cleanup continue to discard it as before; batch lifetime checks still guard restoration. The existing photo simulation checks hair and body collision restoration. In-game physics require verification.

`death_widgets.lua` owns death/revive widget lookup, bounded fallback scanning and the widget/photo-pawn new-object watches. The shared widget state is still reset by the main lifecycle, and guarded references continue to be validated before use. Screen discovery and real engine object lifetimes need an in-game test.

`death_camera.lua` owns held-camera creation, movement, release and diagnostic watching. The main loop calls its interface and discards old camera references on world adoption without touching those actors. It reads current settings per camera operation and shares the existing screen state for photo-mode and clipping interactions. Real camera movement, photo transitions and recovery require a game test.

`death_hud.lua` owns the HUD subtree walk, hiding and restoration while keeping its state on the shared screen object for world cleanup. Live callbacks supply death, manual-ragdoll and tuning state; widget validation, the one-second delay and traversal memoization remain unchanged. The dedicated simulation covers timing, preserved death/revive branches, settings, manual ragdoll, restoration and stale-master handling. In-game widget behavior needs verification.

F9 piece recovery now schedules the equip cloth reset when it undid a piece: the physics remake stopped simulated accessories (yellow suit HandString chain on the forearm twist) and nothing restarted them, so the strap hung dead after F8. `piece`, `clothscan`, `photo`, `f6`, `fall` pass.

Tower F7s flung her 3.4 km to the origin with pelvis 14-28k cm/s before any push and calf 800k cm/s after: stale velocities are now cleared at ragdoll start and F7 stops a flying body before shoving. Spawn ponytail resets are no longer blocked by ambient EnvObj loops (Xion, hill drone, B07 lights); only cinecamera or non-EnvObj sequences block. `f7cloth`, `clothscan`, `cutscene_hair`, `sword_return`, `photo_hold`, `capsule`, `fall` pass.

Fleet fixes ship: gripped weapons snapshot before release, failed weapon restores kept, split copies wake with her half, forced LODs released on early recovery and failed stages, capsule reattach retried. Packaging/docs fixed: uninstall paks, run.sh/README lists, HUD 1 s, P/Face/Outfits, diagonal wording, AGENTS Linux scope, `reviveAt` and `asyncLoad` defaults. `sword_return` (hair-skip + failure-keep), `photo_hold` (zero velocities), `capsule` (socket clear), `split`, `waist`, `f6`, `photo`, `fall` pass.

A sword folded into her hair (SC_HairACC) is now left on its socket through test ragdolls instead of being dropped to a box and restored. Repeated F6/F8 and F10/F8 cycles were reported to pitch the pin-blade higher each time, fixed by F7/F8; the saved relative snapshot across drop/restore is the suspected accumulator. `sword_return` passes; repeated test cycles need an in-game check.

The photo log showed IsGamePaused false while the death tick stopped, with the head drifting 12 cm over one session before close, then capsule jumps, spin-up and caps left behind as stretched skin. The ragdoll hold now zeroes velocities on every hold and re-holds every render frame while photo mode is open, and the photo watch keeps the capsule from following the body. `photo` passes; in-game settling after photo needs a retest.

The latest playtest showed sustained 15–20 ms polls in the first seconds after some deaths, commonly including 4–6 ms attributed to revive-screen handling and 2–4 ms to spin limiting; occasional higher spikes and other work were also present. A death-screen widget can now use a single-object native lifetime check when first queried on a frame rather than checking the entire shared held-object list. It retains per-frame validation, stale-object rejection and the existing UI polling cadence; other held objects keep their normal batching. Six short post-death summaries report pawn ticks per second and mod callback cost, a timing proxy rather than a GPU measurement. `charcache`, `revive_cadence`, `revival_screen`, `reload`, `frame`, `spin_cadence` and `smash_struggle` pass. This targets a measured contributor, not a confirmed explanation of the entire 3–5 second drop.

The latest playtest showed cut-sound steps up to 51 ms, with individual cue lookups taking 12–31 ms, and cap-mesh stages up to 38 ms. World cleanup previously discarded all guarded asset wrappers even though their rooted package assets were retained; the preload queue then skipped those rooted paths. Cleanup now retains only the rooted package-object guards, which are still checked against the native object array before use and discarded on a failed guard. Slow sound guard checks have their own timing line to distinguish that cost from path lookup or loading. The existing 30-second audio preload delay remains because the project records an early-startup audio crash. Ordinary cap geometry now reuses point calculations across the two faces and allocates fewer temporary triangle tables; a local comparison matched all returned geometry values for five outline sizes and took 0.677 s versus 0.861 s for 2,000 representative caps. This benchmark does not establish the in-game reduction or explain every cap spike. `asyncload`, `reload`, `smash_effects`, `cap_fallback`, `frame`, and `stages` pass; actual cut and sound timings require another game test.

When a waist cut occurs after a struggle begins, the original plan could still contain leg actions. The struggle now discards its active action and pulses, rebuilds a waist-only plan, and guards writes to the disconnected lower bodies. Convulsion pulses and gasp jolts queued before the cut are checked again before being applied. The `smash_struggle` simulation exercises an already-running struggle cut at the waist, queued lower-body work, and the existing torso-smash/head-loss cases; `waist`, `struggle` and `smash_lifecycle` also pass. The logs do not establish whether observed motion is commanded or ordinary ragdoll physics, so game verification is still needed.

The latest playtest logged 25–33 ms smash-chunk steps during test smashes, while a combat death separately showed three 24–26 ms mesh-cut stages and a 20 ms cap stage. Smash batches now stop after a chunk when their spawn work reaches 6 ms, resuming the remaining chunks on later impact frames. The existing per-batch maximum, total count, physics and effects remain; a single expensive chunk or mesh load cannot be preempted, and emission may take longer. This does not address the native cut or cap costs and requires an in-game test.

The earlier particle-parking experiment has been reversed for ordinary hit effects and the continuous wound-blood layer, in addition to the already restored stump spurts and smash effects. Native blood-loop and hit assets join the existing deduplicated preload queue. Each continuous wound layer is queued as a separate impact step, rechecks the blood setting, uses the existing opening scale and duration, and is tracked for cleanup. Low-HP cuts retain their existing spurt-only rule. Focused `smash_effects`, `asyncload`, `stages` and `split` simulations pass, including blood-off, effect lifetime and recovery. Real-world appearance, asset loading and frame cost require a playtest.

`bone_lists.lua` owns cached bone trees, sink/carrier exclusions, limb lists, body lists and split-driver lists. The gore lifecycle retains the native command assembly and calls the module’s cache-clear function during world cleanup; tree lifetime remains as before. It reads the current context when querying physics assets and shares the existing enemy state. `cutlist`, `stages`, `split`, `reload` and `smash_hits` pass; real meshes, cleanup and timings require an in-game retest.

Tachy bone component selection and bone UV mapping now live with the other Tachy geometry calculations. `sim_tachy_geometry` uses the shipped core data to check selection, caching and the bone-silver setting; `frame`, `split` and `cap_fallback` also pass. These checks do not establish the rendered result in game.

`settings_manager.lua` owns first-run settings creation, tuning/settings/menu overlays, publishing the current tune, reload notifications and the menu-reset baseline. `main.lua` still owns the live tune variable and passes a setter. `sim_settings_manager` checks that existing settings remain unchanged, overrides and numeric menu paths layer correctly, the reset baseline excludes menu overrides, and reload notifies dependent modules. `menu_layout`, `rebind`, `keys` and `voiceas` pass.

`after_hit_geometry.lua` owns the after-death body-segment and weapon/hand sweep intersection calculations; attack gating and cut dispatch remain in `main.lua`. Its radius and segment helpers are created once instead of on each check. The new `sim_after_geometry` checks a mid-limb hand crossing, a miss and a blade crossing between sampled endpoints; `after`, `after_nomontage`, `swing_perf` and `smash_hits` pass. This does not establish an in-game speedup or real-weapon hit accuracy.

The native harness validates requested group names, fails an explicitly requested real-mesh or physics-parity check if its cache is absent, and retains each parallel real-mesh process exit status. Default runs still report cache-dependent skips without turning them into failures. Unknown-group and missing-cache probes are rejected; the native `alive` check and five cached `head` mesh cases pass.

`death_face.lua` supplies the per-face controller factory to the shared context; each instance retains its own morph cache and active expression. It reads the current tune and gore module through callbacks, preserving settings reload and boss/player usage. `sim_death_face` checks a morph is selected, applied, cleared, and uses new settings on a later start. `voiceas`, `bosses`, `fall` and `reload` pass; actual expression and alternate head compatibility need a game test.

The alternate-character voice simulation previously allowed random vertical splits: a split intentionally cancels all post-death movements and voices, so the test could demand a moan that should not occur. It now configures a limb-only non-smash death with splits disabled while preserving the Tachy, Raven and Eve voice assertions; five focused runs passed. `sim_split` separately checks that split deaths cancel and block post-death movement; it does not directly assert audio output.

`performance.lua` installs frame-reporting and GC-probe functions on the shared screen state while reading the current active/death state, settings and pawn through callbacks. `sim_perf_probe` verifies that even forced GC checks pause during the first six seconds of a death, a collected probe is counted and replaced, and the two fewer-GC options restore their distinct original values. The surrounding `charcache`, `reload`, `asyncload`, `frame` and `ragdoll_probe` simulations pass. This preserves the diagnostic design; it does not measure game frame time.

The previously failing `after_nomontage` simulation now explicitly disables the blunt smash override and proximity-weighted odds for its initial limb-only death, verifies the right arm was cut, and swings across the remaining left arm. It asserts the left arm is cut while retaining walking and overhead-miss checks. The earlier setup could first cut the torso and then sent its positive swing through that torso; another initial limb cut alone did not ensure the old swing was geometrically cuttable. This repairs the fixture, not evidence of an in-game performance change.

`gore_audio.lua` owns gore cue sets and their per-cue, per-poll and global spacing state; `gore_voice.lua` owns voice selection, playback, fade and preload paths. The gore module still exposes the same entry points and provides the current context, object guards, settings and current cut list; the cut list is obtained on demand because cleanup can replace it. `smash_voice`, `voiceas`, `asyncload`, `smash_effects`, `stages`, `split` and `smash_lifecycle` pass. Actual sound timing and any audio-thread crash require an in-game retest.

The native `unit` test runner now checks its process exit and rejects `FAIL` lines in addition to requiring `PASS`. The Windows native unit run passes. `docs/TESTING.md` describes representative fake-engine coverage and what still requires an in-game test.

The default Lua scenario list includes `ragdoll_probe`, which checks that normal deaths skip diagnostic snapshots and that the probe switch restores them; `diag` remains opt-in because diagonal cuts are parked. The runner rejects scenario names containing path separators before removing their scratch folders. The `after_nomontage` failure also reproduces without the newly added overhead-miss check, so that extra check is not its cause.

`object_lifetime.lua` installs `keep`, `held` and the batched `alive_ask` on the shared context. It owns retained-object lists and their per-frame validation epoch; the separate poll epoch is initialized here but advanced by the main loop. The module reads the current tune through a callback so reloaded settings continue to apply. `charcache`, `reload`, `reload_head`, `asyncload`, `newobj_fallback` and `frame` pass; game-engine object lifetime must still be checked in game.

`split_rides.lua` owns the spine anchors permitted for each split half and its lid; the split lifecycle remains in `gore.lua`. Split, lid cadence, cut stage and smash lifecycle simulations pass. The Lua runner now requires a successful process, a PASS line and no FAIL assertion line. The original `after_nomontage` fixture detected a blow whose inferred plane did not result in a cut; restoring pre-geometry-extraction code reproduced it. Forcing the initial death to use limb odds alone did not resolve it. The fixture was subsequently revised as described above.

`cap_geometry.lua` builds the ordinary procedural cap without actor or engine state; `tachy_geometry.lua` installs the existing shared Tachy frame and mesh functions on the gore module, using its settings and vector helpers. Actor creation, lifecycle and materials remain in `gore.lua`. The `frame`, `cap_fallback`, `split`, `stages`, `cape` and `smash_effects` simulations cover the integration; appearance requires an in-game check.

The `slice_response.lua` module parses the native helper’s numeric, vector and opening fields, including outlines. `gore.lua` owns the cut and split lifecycle and loads those pure parsers once; the response format, scaling and failure behavior remain unchanged. Its imports use a temporary scope because the gore chunk is at Lua’s 200-local limit. The `stages`, `split`, `smash`, `after` and `waist` simulations cover the integration.

The November 4 performance audit found that `G` lifetime batches revalidated the same GUObjectArray header and chunk-pointer slots for every object. Those repeated `mem_readable` calls alternate the one-region VirtualQuery cache among the array, chunk-pointer array and item regions. `alive_cmd` now snapshots the checked header and each checked chunk slot once per synchronous batch, while continuing to check each item address, object identity, Unreachable and PendingKill flags; no result is reused across commands or frames. The old object and index checks remain for first-time entries. `test_alive` checks freed, reused and flagged objects as well as the once-per-batch shared checks; `newobj` and Lua character-cache scenarios also pass. This targets redundant work in logged character lifetime batches of 20–113 ms; the cost of that redundancy within the spikes is unknown. Revive-intro opacity sampling was a separate diagnostic-only recursive widget traversal, now enabled with `gore.probes`; intro animation, fade, visibility and audio remain active normally. The revive scenarios pass. Windows `test-native` now passes its scenario arguments to the harness; the earlier attempted focused command inadvertently started the native suite and was interrupted when the wrapper defect was noticed.


The November 4 18:07–18:10 session had two observed real deaths: death-start groups of 69 and 25 ms, limb/torso cut stages of 19–25 ms, an opening of 31 ms, and sound steps of 22 and 30 ms. The HP bar still read above zero at both ragdoll starts; the minute summaries' 109–114 ms lethal-watch maxima cannot be attributed to the killing-hit decision. Character guard batches independently reached 113 ms and the grab watch calls that shared character list, but the records do not prove which subcall caused either maximum. Engine garbage collection coincided with several longer frame hitches. Death-start logs now break out screen, voice, camera/state, physics, outfit/simulation and collision, and slow grab and sound calls have separate timings. Custom configured voice and the current alternate character's voice set join the paced asynchronous preload queue; stock Eve voices were already present. Changing appearance later can still require an on-demand alternate voice load. sim_asyncload covers the custom voice request; stock audio and physics are unchanged. Measured improvements on the observed deaths remain unconfirmed.


Stump blood uses the exact 0.5.5 game Niagara asset `NS_Hit_Flesh_Slice_BloodOnly`, without edits to its emitters or materials. Each opening attaches one component to its cap or bone, with the original 0.6 scale, Z-axis orientation, 0.35 s restart cadence and 2.5 s lifetime (or player tuning). Local smashes use the same scale and duration as 0.5.5. The asset is preloaded, components are reused, split opening spawns are scheduled separately, and restarts are serviced fairly one per render frame, with no extra per-pulse floor traces. Pending restarts can be delayed by cuts and queued impact work; visual continuity and cost need an in-game test. Full-body destruction has no surviving stump. Blood-off and lifecycle cleanup still apply.

The November 4 17:58:26 crash followed two new-pawn adoptions during a reload. The Unreal crash context reports GameThread `EXCEPTION_ACCESS_VIOLATION` executing address zero; the return address at executable +0x1115a5c follows an indirect virtual call at +0x1115a56. The minidump does not include the object memory needed to identify its class, and there is no sbslice crash marker or Lua stack at the fault. It does not establish that the mod caused the crash. A separate code audit found that `forget_world` discarded `introLook` and HUD handles but left `screen.fade` and `screen.rv` (including raw `ghost` widget wrappers) across the world transition; now both are dropped without dereferencing them. `sim_reload` injects a freed old widget into those fields and checks it is never read. This closes that stale-reference path but is not a confirmed fix for this crash.

The same session logged a 128 ms tick containing 65 ms HUD hiding and 52 ms revive-screen work, a 38 ms torso-smash cut stage (including 10 ms opening/ride preparation), and 49 ms batching 65 character lifetime guards. HUD subtree membership is now memoized only within each hide traversal, retaining the same kept branches and visibility handling. A smash no longer parses the invisible detached side's outline; the surviving body opening, caps, blood and regular-cut behavior are retained. Focused HUD/reload and smash tests pass. The timings attribute the full groups, not the individual repeated traversal or outline parsing, so improvement is unmeasured; revive and character-guard spikes remain separate.

Normal ragdoll startup no longer collects the joint-length, cape-length and relative joint-roll snapshots used exclusively by stretch and roll diagnostics. These require many bone transforms and parent reads in the same frame as ragdoll physics; `gore.probes = true` retains the original snapshots and reports. `sim_ragdoll_probe` verifies both normal and diagnostic paths. Their share of the recorded 66 ms setup is unmeasured; the cut stage and death-start costs are separate.

In the 17:46 session, one death spent 66 ms in ragdoll setup (the prior instrumentation did not distinguish its parts). `active.lua` now prepares the nearest physics-parent map for cape bodies one body per alive poll, keyed by both physics layout and skeletal mesh; ragdoll setup reuses the completed map, or finishes it on demand when cold. Mass, joint, spin, cape and weapon behavior is unchanged. Per-stage setup timings now identify where any remaining stalls occur. The same log has 18–33 ms cut stages, 46–61 ms hair-reset/cinematic checks and recurring revive-screen costs; no evidence establishes those as fixed by this change. `sim_cape` compares the cached hierarchy, checks no warm parent reads and verifies a different skeleton rebuilds it.

The November 4 17:22 session logged death selection at 17–22 ms, head cut stages at 27–29 ms and a torso-smash hide at 45 ms. Empty side-driver requests now return before walking the skeleton (the head had no side, yet paid about 6 ms); cut logs omit the large rim coordinate strings while the native result still retains them for geometry. Smash attachment checks memoize repeated socket ancestry within each mesh, and slow hide logs now separate bone writes from attachment scanning. These target measured work without establishing the cause of the 45 ms or removing native cut time. Struggle steering is capped at 30 Hz rather than 60, with lower peak speeds and reflexes; convulsion and neck/arm frequencies and strength are reduced, including a longer interval between headless leg twitches. Real visuals, ragdoll behavior and frame times remain to be tested.

For ordinary deaths, the hit and animated-fall decision are captured during death detection; ragdoll setup runs on the next render frame, and cut selection on the following frame. The pending queue is swapped before execution so callbacks can safely queue the next frame, and death generation guards prevent old callbacks applying to a later death. In the latest session the detection tick spent 14 ms in death setup, 3 ms in hit selection and 15 ms in ragdoll setup (47 ms total); spreading work targets that spike, with up to one additional render frame of cut-selection latency. This does not address the separately observed 37 ms native/Lua cut stage, or the 24–31 ms asset-cache warmups. Measure and visually check in game.

Full-body smashes now default on in tune.lua and settings.default.lua. Projectile callbacks read the actual contacting component first, then the projectile's physics sphere, up to eight created collision shapes, and its root as a fallback. Scaled sphere/capsule radii or equivalent circular cross-sections from the two short box/mesh half-extents become primitive impactRadius data; missing/invalid dimensions remain unknown. Weapon inventories cache the same conservative short-axis cross-section alongside their existing imported bounds, and the selected weapon passes it with the swing-time pose. Long shafts alone cannot imply a broad striking face. Projectile hit/collision/blueprint packets capture the body pose at impact; the generic rock callback reuses the rock-specific snapshot when it is from the same object, mesh and 50 ms window. No new object-list scan, per-frame size query or permanent projectile reference is added.

For known footprints, local targeting compares circular overlap area against the approximate flesh radius around each surviving part's segment, then uses the existing surface/center distance tie-break. Unknown sizes retain the previous nearest-surface targeting and ambiguous-hit variation; absent hit locations retain death-odds fallback. Full-body coverage samples the head, chest, pelvis and both mid-leg regions (14/18/16/10/10 cm radii), combines covered area and requires overlap with both trunk regions. Effective full-body chance is fullBodyChance times coverage squared; the default maximum is 0.5, and a simultaneous volley adds 0.1 per extra impact up to +0.3 (an explicit 0 stays never). Unknown sizes and narrow isolated hits have zero full-body chance, even at a configured maximum of 1. Earlier cuts, a missing torso or fullBody=false prevent promotion. Projectile volley identity/deduplication is retained; simultaneous impacts add a small bonus (0.1 each past the first, up to +0.3) but never bypass the off switch or an explicit 0. The full-body roll remains stable across early/deferred processing. These are circular cross-section and sparse body-coverage estimates, not exact render-mesh contact patches, blast radii or damage-energy simulation. Large colliders may overestimate contact; flat or complex striking meshes can be underestimated. The next game log records radius/source, coverage and effective chance to calibrate them.

sim_smash_area covers defaults, circle geometry, small versus broad local contact, off-center/full-body coverage, invalid and unknown dimensions, captured poses without bone rereads, collider priority, rock/generic data flow, local/full real stages, the full-body off switch and prior cuts. Existing smash/rock/hit/pose/lifecycle/effect/explosion/menu scenarios were checked selectively; the full suite was not run. The new work is confined to projectile hits, weapon discovery and smash selection, but actual frame-time and visual impact remain unmeasured.

The November 4 16:20–16:26 session records three Gorilla rock deaths: left thigh twice and left forearm once. Each rock EventOnHit was followed by the generic projectile callback reporting the same origin; death_hit and hm.lethal_watch accepted that generic packet before reaching the existing rock correction. sw.rock_blow now runs between explosion-show detection and generic projectile fallback in both paths. The known rock callback captures a primitive body pose once per nearby successful impact; sw.rock_hit selects its nearer head/chest point on that pose, and smash_part receives the same pose after ragdoll motion. The generic packet retains its identity, original point, projectile flag and volley count. Conflicting object identities, different meshes, stale events and failed hit results cannot supply this correction. A mesh-hit-only fallback retains the existing short time/distance correlation. This is still the existing head/chest heuristic for those two known rock blueprints, not a new collision-volume reconstruction. Custom Smash Mix, full-body override/chance and other player settings remain authoritative; full-body was disabled in this session. sim_rock exercises both callback orders, low origins, moved ragdolls, lethal-watch/deferred-death selection, torso/full-body stages, identity isolation, misses and expiry.

The same session contains 22–28 ms limb-cut stages, 20–28 ms struggle updates, 15–29 ms actor-pool refills, startup loading stalls, and 47–59 ms find ticks around hair resets and recovery. screen.cloth_tick previously called cloth_meshes before its collision-restoration-only branch, then discarded that result and restored the already-guarded clothColl list. That unused walk now runs only on the branch that actually resets cloth. sim_clothscan checks zero attachment walks while the due collision restore still completes; f7cloth and cutscene_hair cover the surrounding restoration rules. This removes work but does not establish the walk as the entire cause of the logged 49–51 ms restoration ticks, or quantify an FPS improvement. Native calls and the other logged spikes still require measurement and further work; no full test suite is needed for this change.

The Niagara boundary permits the explicit 0.5.5 stump effect and restored smash blood layers. Local smashes queue a scaled blood-only cloud, optional splash and hit blood separately; full-body smashes also queue the existing grinder cloud, deactivating and destroying it. Other non-smash hit/loop systems stay parked. Smash assets are included in the async preload queue, with the shared existing impact queue spreading their work. Blood and smash-burst settings are respected; fake-engine tests cover cloud scale, fallback, switches, full-body cleanup and stump effects. The visual and frame-time results need an in-game test.

Explosion death classification uses a signal from the game's selected death show, not an inferred enemy class or splash radius. mod/ragdoll_spec.json assigns ImpulseLimitVelocity 702 only to EveRD_Explosion1/2; sw.explosion_hit consumes that marker and restores the original limit 700 before rag.start, retaining the game impulse. The blast packet targets the current torso socket and takes precedence over a nearby weapon or stale projectile report. A later confirmed blast clears held blade follow-ups, discards hm.lethal.held from an earlier grab blow, and reopens an earlier blade judgement without regrowing already-cut parts. The existing smash enable/chance, blunt-ragdoll and full-body settings still govern the result. Both rebuilt core assets and Lua must be installed together. sim_blast covers ordinary/animated-marker rejection, restoration, no fabricated nearby-projectile hits, late blade and grab-blow replacement and actual torso smash stages.

The November 4 15:32:57–15:38:58 log classified all five deaths as blades, with no target-aware hit/projectile/death broadcast evidence; one death used a fabricated nearest-enemy blow 623 cm away. That proves a classification fallback, not the identity or radius of the reported explosion. The new death evidence line logs the ragdoll limit, montage and available hit channels to expose any explosion that the game itself routes to a different death category. Corpse-only AoE still requires an actual hit channel; an explosion near a body is not automatically a hit. Stutter evidence includes 49 ms of queued death work, 15–30 ms struggle spikes and a 1,160 ms menu-opening frame; short last-tick reports do not prove a native/render cause. Disabling Niagara removes its loading/spawn/reset work but does not establish zero stutter or explain every remaining hitch.

Swing-sourced blunt smashes measure the impact point against the captured swing-time body pose, falling back to live bones for missing pose entries or projectile hits. The existing torso reroll and full-body settings remain in effect; sim_smash_pose exercises a chest impact after the live ragdoll shifts. Struggle and convulsions cache bone FNames, and active.over_floor reuses the valid Kismet system library object. These avoid repeated lookups rather than changing motion cadence. Recent logs still show expensive smash chunk creation, cut stages, physics updates and frame hitches with short mod ticks; short tick timings alone do not establish the cause of a hitch. In-game profiling and retesting remain necessary.

The November 4 12:39–12:46 session logged recurring `gore lids` (5–13 ms per poll), ragdoll spin caps (2–6 ms per tick), and death-screen revive checks (2–4 ms per poll). Split lids check every fourth tick after 30 still ticks, count skipped ticks toward their follow window, and return to full-rate processing when motion is observed or a new lid is bound. Motion can trail by up to three ticks. Spin and linear-velocity caps remain full-rate because a brief physics spike can occur and disappear between checks; death and revive screens remain full-rate because their visibility can change independently between polls. `sim_lid_cadence`, `sim_spin_cadence`, and `sim_revive_cadence` cover these behaviors. The native cut and copy-mass costs remain unchanged, and actual performance requires in-game measurement.

The November 4 12:04-12:05 manual split session logged 23-53 ms native-cut stages, 34-44 ms per-half lid stages, 48-73 ms opening stages, and about 4.7-4.9 ms per lid-follow update. Native cuts themselves accounted for about 10-13 ms; surrounding Lua/engine calls also contributed. This session used developer splits, so it does not establish performance on natural combat deaths.

Vertical split movement eligibility now uses `gore.split_on(mesh)`: it checks the accepted pending split's original/copy addresses and the completed `split`/`split copy` cut records. `struggle.start`/`tick` and `convulse.start`/`tick` refuse or stop that mesh, including before paused-tick handling. Stopping convulsions drops animated muscle chains and discards queued impulses, faces, gasps and voice events. An unrelated mesh is unaffected; cleanup removes the completed split records. A failed/restored split no longer blocks a later start. Ordinary waist cuts keep their upper-body movements and head loss keeps the existing minor leg behavior.

`split_caps` now resumes a per-half `q.capwork` record. Outline generation, each procedural mesh allocation, skin binding, spine decoration and the neck cap run in separate split dispatches, with `M.posed` set to the saved cut pose on each call. Caps stay hidden until the opening. Copy physics creation, each half's body layout, held-ragdoll outfit rehoming and copy mass setup have separate stages too. `split_layout` resolves the physics body inventory and limb/spine ancestry before the opening; `settle_half` consumes the same classifications, avoids rescanning the inventory for roots with a body, and reads positions only for bodies whose mass may change. Physics simulation, release and separation retain their ordering. Animated falls keep their outfit riders on the original live pose until the existing ragdoll release; only held ragdolls rehome early. These stages add preparation frames to reduce peak work; they do not cap an individual engine call or guarantee an opening-latency improvement.

`flat_lid` projects each world vertex once and resolves the nearest anchor once for a single bending lid. `skin_lid` caches spine segments, uses scalar nearest-segment calculations, and binds only the front face; its matching back vertices share position/weight records and the update mirrors their normals. The live lid signature compares both position and rotation relative to the lid actor, so rigid motion alone no longer triggers a mesh upload; relative bending still does. Existing geometry density, materials, growth, push and follow settings are preserved. Native cut/render synchronization and copy mass/release work can still hitch and need game measurements.

`sim_split` drives the real split dispatcher on the fake engine for an interrupted active arm-loss struggle, pending/completed movement suppression, independent meshes, hidden cap staging, an animated split's ragdoll release, and a game death notification through `main.lua`'s split roll. It also checks ordinary waist movements, cleanup, skin positions/weights and reversed back normals, rigid-motion upload suppression, real bending uploads and body-layout equivalence. Actor allocation and native geometry are simulated; it cannot certify game FPS or PhysX behavior.

The November 4 11:41–11:44 session logged repeated 20–40 ms bone-watch/preload updates, 29–36 ms delayed sound steps, and a low-HP arm opening about 268 ms after its request (including 47 ms cutting and 45 ms accessory-chain stages). The async completion path rooted assets by address but did not populate `load_obj`'s lifetime-guarded Lua wrapper cache, leaving the first sound/effect use to resolve its path. Completed assets now join a separate wrapper-warm queue; `load_obj` resolves and guards each on a quiet update. Wrapper resolution can cost 25–30 ms per update during combat (November 4 13:17–13:18). It remains enabled during combat because delaying it would move this lookup onto the first cut or effect; this cost still needs an in-game solution. Asset identity checks remain in place. Preload paths are deduplicated, pending async checks use a rotating queue with one lookup per update, and requests, checks, wrapper warming, bone scans and actor refills no longer run together in `watch`. Background preparation also pauses whenever a cut or follow-up is busy. The 30-second audio-preload startup gate is retained. `sim_asyncload` checks bounded work, separation from scans, complete rooting/cache warming, timeout fallback and zero asset path scans on first use. These changes remove repeated first-use lookup work; they do not eliminate native mesh/render synchronization or prove a measured FPS gain.

Opening sounds previously waited for pending and queued cuts and earlier blood/meat jobs. Ready opening sounds now receive priority on the next pump, which returns after audio. Opening sounds and killing-blow wound cues bypass the global 0.35 s apart gate, while the per-window duplicate guard remains; the log records playback attempts and suppression, not proof of playback. `sim_smash_effects` checks priority, a recent gore cue and the existing effect and sound guards. Actual audio timing, native cut costs and frame times require an in-game check.

The 20:21–20:31 session ended in an access violation on the audio mixer render thread (write to address `0x70`) whose last mod line played smash sound layers; the lethal hit's crunch had played 134 ms earlier. No other mod work ran between the two. The 0.6.4 revert to the pre-0.6.3 sound lookup and same-update layers is retained, and `sfx()` now additionally refuses to start a cue twice in one poll window, which forced layers could do past the apart gate. `sim_smash_effects` checks per-window single starts and full selection across windows. The audio-thread stack does not establish the mod as the cause, and this needs an in-game test.

The 23:14–23:55 session's polls cost 6–9 ms each (enemies ~3.2, swing ~1.6, death-screen revive ~1.0–1.5), about a quarter of a 120 fps frame budget every 33 ms, plus per-minute slow ticks of 34–102 ms (character find, swing, guard, lethal, hits, recover, face) and 90–1349 ms frame hitches, most with the mod's last tick at 0–2 ms. Her watches, the enemies' part and the poll each run on a frame of their own, and each walked the whole character list with its lifetime checks: two walks of every three were wasted on the same game state. `live_chars` now walks once per poll window (`goreCtx.poll`, advanced per window in `game_tick`) and shares the list across the window's phases; positions still refresh through `char_at`'s own clock cache and every consumer still guards dying actors. Held-object lifetime checks intentionally stay on the per-frame epoch because the engine runs between phases and can free actors there. `sim_charcache` checks the shared walk, batching after pauses, and zero revalidation for later phases of the same window. Single-nearest lookups skip the full sort. In-game frame times are not yet measured.

Death-face morph weights are applied every other poll: the weights ease on wall-clock curves over half a second or more, so a 66 ms step is invisible, while a face wrote a hundred or more morph targets a poll (an 89 ms spike in the 23:14–23:55 session). No simulation covers the visual result; this needs an in-game check.

Per-minute "find 34–42 ms" ticks while playing traced to the post-respawn cloth reset on the tick frame: the hair scan walked up to 32 parent bones per hair bone every reset. Each bone's first Bip001 ancestor is now resolved once per mesh asset (the physics layout's own cache invalidates it), and only simulating state is re-asked. `sim_clothscan` drives two resets plus a state flip and a new mesh. The same audit moved the death-HUD widget-tree walk (12–58 ms on cut-opening frames) from 0.3 s to 1 s into the death, past every cut and split stage, and made character-list seeds wait for the first poll with no cut opening (15–69 ms object-array walks). Seed backfill still catches what the spawn watch missed; `sim_charcache` checks the wait and the catch-up. Seed and respawn hitches adjoining level loads remain, as does the native cut cost (22–50 ms per `sbslice c`).

The last session logged death-screen hook setup at 48–65 ms repeatedly while its class was loaded. Hook setup now keeps a native-lifetime-checked class reference after the first lookup and reacquires it if it goes away, while retaining the same once-a-second hook registrations. `sim_hook_perf` checks all six registrations and invalidation/reacquisition. This eliminates repeated explicit class scans; the remaining `RegisterHook` cost and in-game improvement are not yet measured.

The 23:08 low-health hit spent 64 ms in the arm watch while selecting from 30 fast weapon-sweep steps. The nearest-point search now computes the same sampled segment projections with scalar values, allocating a position only when it is the nearest so far; body target points for a step are also shared between its weapons. Sampling density, score, age and movement-away penalties are unchanged. `sim_swing_perf` compares twelve 30-step trails with the original vector calculation, including the selected part and point. Its local run took 0.010 s versus 0.030 s for the reference calculation; these are simulation timings, not game frame times.

The latest session repeatedly logged bone scans (5–8 ms) and actor-pool refills (10–18 ms) in the same update. Refill is now scheduled for the following watch update, with cut and async-load conditions rechecked before spawning. This retains the pool targets and spawning behavior while separating their costs. The attachment inventory is diagnostic only and now runs on cut opening only when `verboseLog` or `gore.probes` is enabled. `sim_asyncload` verifies the refill and scan occur in separate frames; actual game frame times remain unmeasured.

The 23:03–23:08 session recorded 347 ms walking 50 held characters immediately after a long pause. Guards could still be marked listed after their last request expired: the first `held()` pruned the others, then each returning guard rebuilt and revalidated the growing list. `live_chars` now batches every character not yet checked in the current frame, including listed-but-expired guards, before walking them. The character-cache simulation covers this case. Windows recorded an access violation at executable offset `0x53da297`; the dump shows stack exhaustion in a recursive game function (return address `0xe6de37` repeats hundreds of times), after an animation pose with 181 entries was asked for index 193. A settled limb was copied before the crash, but the evidence does not establish that this caused the mismatch. The session's CH_P_EVE_59 reference skeleton has 220 bones with valid parent ordering, and the native harness passes seven left-arm checks on its mesh, including posed, driven-helper and 45-degree cuts. A separate simulation verifies stump placement under a moving and rotating attachment bone. These checks do not reproduce the live animation worker or the visible displacement; the crash and misplaced stumps remain unresolved.

The 22:53–22:58 session recorded a 283 ms character-list interval without a fresh character or laser scan, split stages of 22–60 ms, and repeated split-lid updates of 5–17 ms. Character-list logs now break slow intervals down into seeding, batched checks, and walking held objects. Split-lid preparation reuses inverse bone rotations, and detailed-lid noise avoids creating helper closures per sample. The session had `gore.probes` disabled, so it did not capture stretched-triangle diagnostics; the visual cause remains unconfirmed. Other frame hitches were not all attributable to the mod.

Tachy cut-surface generation reuses each outline point's projection and angle across its rings and reuses identical lattice noise samples. This leaves mesh density, generated geometry and material coordinates unchanged while reducing repeated Lua calculations on a cut frame. In-game performance still needs measurement.

The 22:20–22:33 playtest logged character-list stalls up to 281 ms, repeated floor-decal lookups of 12–20 ms, cut stages of 15–39 ms, and frame hitches coinciding with garbage collection. `live_chars` now batches unlisted indexed as well as fresh character guards before walking them, avoiding repeated full held-list validation when guards return after aging out. `load_obj` reuses rooted assets only after the existing native identity guard succeeds; stale guards are discarded and assets looked up again. This extends the guarded floor-material cache to effects and sounds. Simulation checks cannot establish in-game latency or rule out engine GC, actor creation, cap mesh and audio stalls. A 0.6.3 report of freezing while attacked on the ground with the low-HP arm option enabled has no stack or reproduction here; lowarm and death/quit simulations pass, but the cause is not established.

The Keys tab already supports changing the menu binding. `keys.lua` registers all eight Ctrl/Shift/Alt combinations for each supported key and dispatches the most specific combination seen per frame; the three-modifier combination was previously missing. The binding and menu state still need in-game verification with UE4SS and other mods.

Sandbox weapons: the death watch now gives visible weapon-slot or weapon-named components of a boss spawned here an invisible simulated box based on its imported bounds, releases any master pose, and attaches the weapon to the box. It records the parent, socket, relative transform and master pose, restores them on actor reuse/removal, and rolls back a failed attachment; a vanished/destroyed world is not touched. Components without usable bounds or attachment are skipped and logged. `sim_corpse` covers dropping, restoring and rollback; actual weapon skeletons, poses, collisions and visibility still require a game test. Story bosses and regular enemies retain their previous handling.

The installed `TachyNaked_P` overrides `CH_M_NA_21_Phase1_Body`; inspecting that asset shows it references `CH_M_NA_21_Phase1_Body_PhysicsAsset`, a separate asset the mod container does not include, and the vanilla paks ship no Phase1 physics asset either. Assigning the game's regular `CH_M_NA_21_Body_Physics` does not match this body setup: the tested ragdoll still stopped immediately and previous attempts to restart it stretched the body. `bosses.lua` now detects a Phase1 mesh at adoption, leaves its physics alone, marks it unsupported, and skips the death struggle and any restart; the death watch still logs the physics state. `sim_bosses` covers the mark and the skipped struggle. Whether the game can ragdoll this body at all remains unconfirmed in game.

Prologue Tachy is now a Sandbox Boss kind (`tachynpc`, CharacterTable ID 20020, row `N_TachyNPC`). Her row is an allied NPC row: it applies `Immortal_Infinite`, uses the Player death alias and has `bApplyDamageWhenPlayerAlive` false. `prepare` swaps a kind's own immortality pair (`k.immortal`: hers is `Immortal_Infinite` to `Immortal_Infinite_Dispel`, verified to exist in the EffectTable first; without that dispel the spawn is refused and the row untouched), sets the damage flag while one of yours is up, and otherwise treats her like the other bosses (ragdoll death rows, scaled HP, kept corpse). Everything is put back exactly on forget. Her `N_TachyNPC_AI` detect task requires `Check_AttackTachyNPC` and `Check_Detect` on targets, so whether she actually engages Eve is unverified: she may stand until cut like a passive boss. Her faction fields are deliberately untouched. `sim_bosses` covers the refusal, the mortal setup and the exact restore. Spawn her in game and report whether she fights, stands, or needs her detect effects; that decides what (if anything) comes next.

For the report of frantic, unnatural movement, the default struggle uses lower limb speed and acceleration, softer rocking, dragging, breath and stump reflexes, and a smaller initial surge. Convulsion arm swings and neck strains are less frequent, slower and gentler; gasp lifts and the struggle's final tremors and head roll are reduced. The existing struggle strength and length settings still apply, and boss struggles use the same tuning. This is a provisional visual adjustment, not a diagnosis of a particular animation or physics fault; simulations check the movement lifecycle, while appearance and whether the reduced forces remain visible require in-game testing.

The 12:09 playtest logged enemy swing tracking up to 164 ms and boss checks averaging 4.1 ms per poll, alongside separate Play As voice and engine GC hitches. Weapon trail endpoints now share one component transform read instead of reading it twice. Boss HUD lookup and actor distance checks run only when at least one recently hit live boss has no HP reading; nearest-boss selection still includes all live bosses when the fallback is needed. Boss and after-death simulations cover the existing fallback and combat paths. In-game timing and visual behavior remain to be verified; these changes do not address all observed stalls. The same test showed a custom Tachy phase-one body with no reported physics asset: its pelvis was simulating at death but stopped within 0.1 s and the actor was adopted again. Prior attempts to restart its ragdoll caused severe stretching, so this evidence does not establish a safe fix. The NPC Tachy assets include prologue attack skills, but their AI/spawn setup has not yet been established.

Vertical split validation sends the source component's hidden LOD0 material slots to the native cut. The cut still processes all sections, but reports visible vertex, vanished and unsunk totals separately; the fallback decision uses those totals. This targets the custom Tachy and Scarlet playtests, where the copied half repeatedly reported 34,633 and 96,651–144,559 unsunk vertices, respectively, while Play As hid embedded hair. The exact hidden-section contribution and resulting split geometry need in-game confirmation; visible unsunk vertices still trigger the fallback. Material slots above 63 are conservatively counted as visible.

The current working version is 0.7.0. The Lua entry point supplies the version shown at startup and used for native DLL version metadata and release packaging.

A re-run of the Player death-show writes on the same table object that finds a show no longer ours means another mod rewrote it after us (death-sequence mods in collections such as DEGEN AIO); the re-run names the fields (`tables.lua` `fought`, `recheck_dead` after a ragdoll-less non-scripted death). This detects the fight but does not resolve load-order crashes; the conflicting mods must be disabled.

At startup the mod rotates its current log to `.old`. If rotation fails, it opens the current log in append mode instead of truncating it; the installer also carries `.old` across reinstalls. This preserves available evidence but does not establish the cause of the reported outfit-dependent death crash or long loading delay.

Mesh copies made by `clone_of` inherit the source's hidden material sections per LOD as well as its materials. This preserves Play As's hidden embedded hair on split, photo and regrow copies; section visibility is queried by material ID, as in Play As. `sim_regrow` checks the state on two LODs. The split-only odds test ignores a saved diagonal weight when the diagonal feature is disabled, matching the death roll; `sim_smash_hits` covers enabled and disabled cases. Neither change establishes the cause of the reported Scarlet split failure.

The 19:29 full-body volley created 64 pieces in batches of two and logged a source in mid-air; the normal settled-body tracker could also make a large pool under the now-invisible pelvis. Full-body smashes now snapshot twelve body-bone positions before hiding, distribute pieces across them, follow pelvis displacement as before, and create up to four pieces per batch. This reduces the duration and point-source appearance but cannot guarantee that all later pieces track a fast-moving body. Full-body blood adds the vanilla `Grinder_Dead/NS_A_BloodExplosive` Niagara system to the existing flesh burst and hit effect, scheduled separately and preloaded with smash assets; it honors blood/burst switches. The full-body path suppresses death-hit, cut and smash splats as well as the settled pelvis tracker; only up to the configured tracked chunk count creates pools, when the pieces settle, without moving-chunk drips. Earlier independent hit decals and Niagara's own rendered particles are not controlled by that pool rule. Normal smashes retain their existing behavior. `smash_effects` covers these paths against fake effects and chunk physics, and lifecycle tests cover cleanup; visual density, native Niagara behavior and the cost of four physics actors per batch need an in-game test. A playtest then showed the grinder cloud lingering indefinitely instead of going away after its few seconds; DeactivateImmediate is not on this build's component, so the component is stopped with Deactivate and destroyed, and stop/destroy failures are logged. The same test showed no visible pools under the meat: the fixed small pools hid under their own chunks, so each tracked chunk's pool is now sized past its chunk and starts at half size. A follow-up test showed most chunks still pool-less, so every chunk of a full-body smash is now tracked on the shared creation/check/growth budgets (local smashes keep the first-few cap). A further test showed pools snapping in one by one over a stuttering death: each first-use asset resolution cost a full object scan on the game thread (12-48 ms), repeated per pool while the preload queue had not reached the pool materials. Pool materials now preload first, broken async deliveries fall back to sync after 5 s, the killing blow pre-requests still-unrooted death blood over async, pool creation runs up to four per pass now that each costs about a millisecond rooted, and chunk pools start at a visible size on a fast growth curve while body pools keep the slow one. In-game frame cost, hitch behavior and appearance still need verification.

The 19:21 test logged two nearby Blueprint hit callbacks before death and none afterward. The killing hit was classified as projectile but picked the forearm as the nearest part; its normal full-body override was off. A bounded 0.35-second history now counts distinct projectile IDs or object identities, filters same-key and nearby cross-hook duplicates, and gives two or more distinct reports a full-body smash while the body is intact. It updates retained hit records so a lethal hit saved before the next report can also see the volley. Identity is now logged for Blueprint hits; the old log cannot establish whether its two callbacks were distinct projectiles. Blueprint proximity is not proof of damage, and the game may report fewer impacts than were visible. Events after death can now queue during the initial delay, though execution still waits for it; duplicate, queue, expiry, pipeline and hit-limit guards remain. The implementation adds work on projectile events, not a new per-frame scan. `smash_hits` simulates volley selection, duplicate and stale reports, full-body recovery and early post-death queuing; actual event ordering and behavior require an in-game test. A smash already started or an earlier cut cannot be converted into a full-body smash.

When the game's death show has the animated-fall marker, a recognized projectile or rock hit now leaves the already-running death ragdoll on. This also uses the lethal watch's saved projectile for a delayed show, independently of whether Blunt Smash is enabled; the explicit animated-death test key remains animated. `animdeath` checks a reported projectile hit with smash disabled and an ordinary marked-death control. This depends on the existing projectile detection and on the death reaching the ragdoll/show or existing no-show fallback; not every projectile or scripted death path is established in-game.

The 19:05–19:07 tests logged nearby Blueprint projectile hit events at the killing blows and subsequent lethal smashes, but no post-death projectile events. The former Hits After Death path sampled weapon swings only. Reported target-checked collision/network hits and proximity-filtered Blueprint hits now enter a queue only during an eligible death with both Hits After Death and Blunt Smash enabled. It holds at most four short-lived primitive hit records, filters duplicate callbacks, waits for a free gore pipeline, and shares the existing six-hit default limit and initial delay; a hit more than 55 cm from the nearest surviving target is rejected. A split remains ineligible. There is no new per-frame object scan; existing event hooks and the existing post-death tick are reused. Blueprint events lack a target and proximity can misclassify nearby impacts. Whether rockets keep delivering any relevant event after Eve dies remains unverified, and an unreported collision cannot be turned into a smash by this path.

In the 18:53 thigh-smash test, the only body-opening pool logged 56 cm across; `on_settled` skipped its larger body tracker whenever any decal or tracker existed, including small chunk pools. It now starts a pelvis tracker unless the same pawn already has a non-chunk tracker with at least the configured body radius; the existing wall/ring checks, lifetime and decal budget still apply. The same test logged a 4 cm head shift at photo-mode exit, then body wake-ups and a hand reaching 2161 cm/s. It does not establish what made the body stretch. Photo-mode collision snapshots now include the main body mesh and restore its collision enabled state and channel responses before the ragdoll hold is released, rather than only restoring the other meshes afterward. `photo` and `smash_effects` simulations cover the restoration and body-pool eligibility; actual physics and appearance require an in-game retest.

Three startup minidumps at 18:31 and 18:45 showed the same UE4SS 3.1.0-6 `set_bool`/hook-dispatch stack immediately after ConsoleEnablerMod unregistered its `ClientRestart` hook inside the callback. In the pinned UE4SS source, `UnregisterHook` erases and frees that callback's `LuaUnrealScriptFunctionData`; the pre-hook dispatcher then accesses `lua_data.lua` again to clear the game-thread flag. This is a use-after-free consistent with the crash, though the dumps do not prove every individual crash's identity. The installer changes only the pinned stock console script to leave that hook registered; after console creation its existing guard makes it dormant. A byte-for-byte backup is retained and a customized script is not modified. Launch reliability still requires repeated game testing.

Stump spurts queue two small floor decals at each emission. The game's `NS_HitBlood_PC_01` uses GPU-simulated particles and the Lua path has no individual particle collision positions; the dots are an approximation scattered around the source, not actual particle-impact locations. Each queued spot is processed separately, traces to the floor, requires a reasonably upward-facing surface, respects `blood`, `pool` and `maxDecals`, and has no engine lifetime. Burst dots are exempt from decal-budget recycling and are destroyed during the existing recovery cleanup; at the cap no new dots are added. Enemy-owned dots follow the existing enemy cleanup. `smash_effects` checks floor placement, budget protection and recovery cleanup, not in-game appearance or frame cost.

In the 0.6.3 turret-shot test the projectile hooks registered, but no projectile collision was recorded before the death; the mod selected a limb cut. New-object watching now also hooks `EventOnHit` on loaded projectile Blueprint classes, accepting a successful hit within 45 cm of Eve's sampled bones as a possible projectile impact. This event has no target parameter: it can misclassify a nearby hit on something else and does not establish turret, explosion or general projectile coverage. `smash_hits` checks filtering and classification with fake engine calls. Blueprint inheritance, hook installation, delivery and ordering need an in-game test.

The same test found neither visible widening nor directional change from component scaling and rotation of `NS_A_BloodLoop`.
